# Freemium Strategy in Competitive App Markets: Maintaining Profitability with Product Fit Uncertainty

Xiaoxiao Luo<sup>1</sup>, Jie Zhang<sup>2</sup>, Minqiang Li<sup>3</sup>

<sup>1</sup>Southwestern University of Finance and Economics, China, luoxx@swufe.edu.cn <sup>2</sup>College of Business Administration, The University of Texas at Arlington, USA, jiezhang@uta.edu <sup>3</sup>College of Management and Economics, Tianjin University, China, mqli@tju.edu.cn

## Abstract

Today’s app market is highly competitive and rapidly growing. This study considers two app developers with substitutable apps and a continuum of consumers with heterogeneous preferences. Each developer decides between offering a paid app and offering a free basic app with in-app purchases. Because of product fit uncertainty, consumers are not sure about the degree of misfit between the app and their preferences, and that uncertainty can be reduced by trying the free basic app. Using a game-theoretic modeling framework, we analyze how product fit uncertainty affects competing developers’ profits and examine the scenario in which each developer offers a paid app or a free basic app with in-app purchases in a duopoly setting. The analytical results suggest that the developer can offer a free basic app for consumer learning even if the app is not underestimated. When either developer offers a free trial, the developer with the higher-quality app will prefer higher product fit uncertainty, but the one with the lower-quality app will favor lower product fit uncertainty. Additionally, as the app quality increases or consumer preferences become stronger, developers may switch the strategy depending on the reduction in product fit uncertainty caused by the free trial. Our study establishes the usefulness of the freemium strategy beyond the contexts analyzed in the literature. The results explain empirical observations of the app market and provide recommendations about the information disclosure of fit attributes in a competitive market.

Keywords: Product Fit Uncertainty, Consumer Learning, Freemium Strategy, Duopoly Market, In-App Purchases

Kenny Cheng was the accepting senior editor. This research article was submitted on July 7, 2020 and underwent three revisions. Minqiang Li is the corresponding author.

## 1 Introduction

In 2021, over 230 billion global app downloads generated \$170 billion in consumer spending.<sup>1</sup> Given this very high level of consumer spending, the app market is both attractive and competitive for app developers (hereafter referred to as developers). The vice president of Rovio, which released Angry Birds, said that “the app economy is the most competitive marketplace that’s ever existed.” <sup>2</sup> As of the first quarter of 2021, there were 2.22 million apps on Apple’s App Store and 3.48 million apps on Google Play,<sup>3</sup> but the average user only had 40 apps installed on their mobile device.<sup>4</sup> Therefore, apps compete with each other, trying to be the one among many that is eventually chosen by the consumer.

Considering the large selection of available apps on the market, it is easy to find apps with specific functionalities that can satisfy basic needs. Thus, users pay much attention to apps’ fit attributes. For example, there are hundreds of weather apps on Apple’s App Store. While each of these displays weather information such as the temperature, humidity, and barometric pressure, they present the information in different ways to cater to the preferences of various consumers.

Since the evaluation of a fit attribute is subjective and varies by individual, consumers cannot learn much about how well app attributes fit based on other users’ experiences (Markopoulos & Clemons, 2013). The product page is another source of information about the product’s attributes, where developers introduce features, functionalities, and the user interface via descriptions, screenshots, and preview.<sup>5</sup> Nevertheless, considering that apps are experience goods, developers cannot perfectly describe all characteristics or convey the feel of using an app via the product page (Shapiro & Varian, 1998). For example, characteristics such as the writing style of book apps, the recommendation mechanism of music apps, and the excitement produced by playing game apps are things that consumers must experience to evaluate. Therefore, product fit uncertainty (Hong & Pavlou, 2014), which reflects consumers’ difficulties in assessing whether a product’s attributes match their own preferences, is particularly salient in the app market.

In addition to presenting detailed product information, developers are encouraged to offer free trials to increase experience-based consumer learning and reduce product fit uncertainty. <sup>6</sup> When a developer offers a free basic app with in-app purchases, users can download the basic app and pay to unlock in-app purchases for premium features if the basic app matches their preferences. For example, on the popular entertainment app Barbie Dreamhouse Adventures, users can create basic Barbie Dreamhouse experiences in the free basic app and can then unlock in-app purchases with additional characters and activities if they find the basic app to be interesting. The alternative to offering a free basic app with in-app purchases is a paid app. In this case, users pay to download the entire app without any experience-based learning prior to purchase. For example, BusyBox sells for \$2.99 and includes all functionalities.

Although the app store recommends that developers disclose app information on their product pages and facilitate consumer learning through free trials, developers have contradictory views about the impact of reducing product fit uncertainty and the effect of free trial offers (Liu et al., 2014). First, as product fit uncertainty decreases, consumers become more certain about their preferences regarding the product, resulting in higher valuations for some users and lower valuations for others. Considering larger consumer heterogeneity, app developers have greater difficulties in capturing the consumer surplus. Second, offering free trials leads to the cannibalization effect. Some users find it sufficient to use the basic app only and choose not to buy the in-app purchases, which may shrink the number of paying consumers and hurt the developer’s profit. Third, the reduction of product fit uncertainty has a public-good characteristic, which further complicates the developer’s decision in a competitive market. The trial experience of one app increases product familiarity (Johnson & Russo, 1984), thereby helping consumers identify the key attributes of the product category and find the app with the best fit (Hong & Pavlou, 2014).

Considering the App Store’s suggestions for reducing product fit uncertainty and the developer’s concerns regarding its negative impacts, it is unclear how the reduction in product fit uncertainty would affect the developer’s profit and whether or not the developer should offer a free trial in a competitive market. Therefore, the primary goal of this study is to examine the following research questions: (1) How does product fit uncertainty affect an app developer’s profit in a competitive market? (2) Under what conditions will an app developer choose to offer a free basic app with in-app purchases rather than offering a paid app?

In this study, we develop a game-theoretic model to consider two competing developers with substitutable apps and a continuum of consumers with heterogeneous preferences. Each developer decides to either offer a paid app or offer a free basic app with inapp purchases and also determines the corresponding price of the paid app or the price of in-app purchases. In the presence of product fit uncertainty, consumers are not sure about the degree of misfit between the app and their preferences. After trying the basic app (if offered), consumers can make purchase decisions based on their updated valuations. Using this framework, we analyze how product fit uncertainty affects the developer’s profit and examine when the developer should offer a free basic app with in-app purchases rather than a paid app.

Our study establishes the usefulness of the freemium strategy beyond the contexts analyzed in previous studies, providing a new perspective to explain the usefulness of the freemium strategy for consumer learning. Previous studies (Chellappa & Shivendu, 2005; Cheng & Liu, 2012; Dey et al., 2013; Dey & Lahiri, 2016; Niculescu & Wu, 2014; Wei & Nault, 2013) have found that offering a lower-functionality version of the product to reduce product uncertainty can be optimal in certain conditions if the product is underestimated in a monopoly market. However, they have not examined whether to adopt the freemium strategy for consumer learning if the product is not underestimated in a competitive market. Our research finds that the freemium strategy can be the equilibrium strategy when product fit uncertainty is considered in a duopoly market where the free trial does not change average consumer valuations.

Our study also generates several interesting findings that are unique to the literature. First, when the developers both offer paid apps, they always benefit from lower product fit uncertainty. However, when either developer offers a free basic app with in-app purchases, their profits are not monotonic in product fit uncertainty. The developer with the higher-quality app prefers higher product fit uncertainty, whereas the developer with the lower-quality app favors lower product fit uncertainty. Second, as the app quality or the strength of consumer preference changes, developers may switch their strategy, depending on the reduction in product fit uncertainty caused by the free trial. Specifically, a higher app quality or a lower strength of consumer preference may decrease the number of free basic apps in the market when offering the free trial significantly reduces product fit uncertainty, but the reverse may occur when the free trial only slightly reduces product fit uncertainty. Third, when two developers adopt different strategies in symmetric situations, the developer who offers a free basic app with in-app purchases always earns a lower profit than the rival who offers a paid app.

The rest of the paper is organized as follows. We present a review of the related literature in Section 2. Section 3 introduces the general model framework. The equilibrium results are analyzed and discussed in Section 4. Section 5 extends the main model in four directions. Finally, we conclude the paper in Section 6.

## 2 Literature Review

Our study is related to the literature on consumer learning. Initially proposed by Arrow (1963), product uncertainty refers to the difficulty for consumers to accurately predict the value of an experience good before using it themselves. Previous studies have investigated various methods adopted by firms to reduce product uncertainty or to facilitate consumer learning (Hao & Tan, 2019; Li et al., 2019; Markopoulos & Clemons, 2013; Markopoulos & Hosanagar, 2018), including publishing product advertisements (Feng & Xie, 2012; Hao, 2000), presenting product reviews (Chen & Xie, 2008; Kwark et al., 2014; Liu et al., 2017; Yi et al., 2019), containing various types of information in user interfaces (Xu, 2018; Yi et al., 2015), and offering lower-functionality versions (Chellappa & Shivendu, 2005; Cheng & Liu, 2012; Dey et al., 2013; Dey & Lahiri, 2016; Lahiri & Dey, 2018; Niculescu & Wu, 2014; Wei & Nault, 2013). While the former three streams of literature have focused on consumer learning via information offered by the firm or consumers, the latter stream has examined consumer learning via personal experiences. Since this study analyzes when a developer should offer a basic app to reduce product fit uncertainty, Table 1 compares our study with the related literature on experience-based consumer learning.

Previous studies (Chellappa & Shivendu, 2005; Cheng & Liu, 2012; Dey et al., 2013; Dey & Lahiri, 2016; Niculescu & Wu, 2014; Wei & Nault, 2013) have found that a firm may offer a feature-limited or timelimited version to reduce product uncertainty when it is underestimated. That is, when the free trial increases average consumer valuations, offering the basic version as a product sample can be an optimal strategy, enabling the firm to charge a higher price for the full version. Different from the above studies, Lahiri and Dey (2018) proposed a new perspective to explain the effectiveness of the versioning strategy when the product is not underestimated. They found that the presence of an informed segment of consumers changes the market information structure. Thus, the versioning strategy can be optimal even if the average willingness to pay remains the same after product exposure. Our study offers another perspective to explain why using the basic version to inform consumers can be a profitable strategy when the product is not underestimated. In our settings, the free trial reduces product fit uncertainty, leading to more heterogeneous valuations but identical average willingness to pay. A larger range of consumers’ expected perceived utility differences between two apps decreases product substitutability and softens the competition effect, which benefits developers in a duopoly market.

Our study is also related to the sparse literature on freemium strategy in competitive markets. Nan et al. (2016) focused on the duopoly setting, where one firm uses the feature-limited freemium strategy and its rival uses the seeding freemium strategy. They explored how the network intensity, quality level, and seeding ratio affect the competing firms’ prices and profits. Zhang et al. (2016) examined the impact of the network effects on the adoption of the freemium strategy in a competitive market, where each firm chooses between offering a free core product with a paid service or offering the core product and the paid service as a bundle. Xu et al. (2018) further studied the duopoly setting, where each firm adopts either the freemium or bundling strategy, and they found that the complementary effect is another key factor that would affect the choices of the competing firms. To conclude, the previous studies in this field examine how quality level, network effect, complementary effect, and other factors affect the design of freemium strategy in a duopoly setting, but they do not model the product uncertainty. Our work contributes to the literature by studying the impact of product fit uncertainty on the freemium strategy in a competitive market. With our unique model settings, we obtain several novel results, some of which are presented below. First, when the free trial reduces product fit uncertainty, the freemium strategy leads to a competition-softening effect beyond the cannibalization effect. Thus, the freemium strategy becomes a possible better choice. Second, counterintuitively, the developer’s profit is not monotonic in product fit uncertainty when either developer offers a free basic app with in-app purchases. Third, the impact of the app quality or the strength of consumer preference on the developer’s equilibrium strategy may depend on the reduction in product fit uncertainty caused by the free trial.

## 3 Model

We consider a market with two competing developers: Developer 1 and Developer 2. Each developer offers one app to consumers, which comprises basic functionalities and premium features. One of the two following strategies would be adopted: (1) Offering a paid app, under which users pay to enjoy the basic functionalities as well as the premium features; (2) offering a free basic app with in-app purchases, whereby the basic functionalities are offered for free and the premium features are sold as in-app purchases. Regardless of the developer’s strategy, we use whole app to represent apps that include the basic functionalities as well as the premium features.

Given the two optional strategies for each developer, there are four possible market configurations: (1) Case PP, where both developers offer paid apps; (2) Case FP, where Developer 1 offers a free basic app with inapp purchases and Developer 2 offers a paid app; (3) Case PF, where Developer 1 offers a paid app and Developer 2 offers a free basic app with in-app purchases; and (4) Case FF, where both developers offer free basic apps with in-app purchases.

The two apps are differentiated in terms of their quality attributes and fit attributes. Quality attributes determine the intrinsic value of the app and fit attributes affect the misfit cost of the app. We assume that a consumer’s utility toward an app is equal to the app’s intrinsic value minus the consumer’s misfit cost. The intrinsic value of whole app ?? and basic app ?? (?? ∈ {1,2}) is represented by $v _ { i }$ and $v _ { i b } .$ , respectively, where $v _ { i } > v _ { i b }$ . The misfit cost is equal to the product of the unit misfit cost and the degree of misfit. Since using all the basic functionalities and the premium features incurs a higher misfit cost than using only the basic functionalities, we assume that the unit misfit cost (the strength of consumer preference) of using whole app ?? is higher than that of using basic app $t _ { b } , \mathrm { i . e . , } t > t _ { b } . ^ { 7 }$

Consumers are uniformly distributed on a unit line (Hotelling, 1929), with Developer 1 located at 0 and Developer 2 located at 1. A consumer’s location ?? represents the consumer’s ideal app, and the developer’s location represents the app the developer produces. Thus, the degree of misfit toward app ?? is the distance between the consumer and developer ??. That is, ?? and 1 − ?? represent the consumer’s actual degree of misfit toward App 1 and App 2, respectively. Therefore, the actual utilities of the consumer with ?? are as follows:

$$
\begin{array}{r l} & V _ {1} = v _ {1} - t y, \\ & V _ {2} = v _ {2} - t (1 - y), \\ & V _ {1 b} = v _ {1 b} - t _ {b} y, \\ & V _ {2 b} = v _ {2 b} - t _ {b} (1 - y), \end{array}\tag{1}
$$

where $V _ { 1 }$ and $V _ { 2 }$ are the consumer’s actual utilities of Whole App 1 and Whole App 2, respectively, and $V _ { 1 b }$ and $V _ { 2 b }$ are the consumer’s actual utilities of Basic App 1 and Basic App 2, respectively.<sup>8</sup>

When consumers have objective and universal measures of quality attributes, it is easy to compare the quality attributes of different apps through consumer reviews or third-party reviews. However, the preference for an experience good is the consumer’s private information, which varies across individuals (Nelson, 1970; Shapiro, 1983). Therefore, we assume that consumers know the app quality or its intrinsic value but are uncertain about their own misfit or how the app fits their specific tastes (Ma & Kauffman, 2014; Markopoulos & Clemons, 2013). <sup>9</sup> Although consumers do not know their actual misfit, they share common prior beliefs about the distribution of their actual degree of misfit. Following previous studies (Kwark et al., 2014; Kwark et al., 2017; Johnson & Myatt, 2006; Lewis & Sappington, 1994), we assume that consumers get a signal of misfit $s _ { k }$ in case ?? $( k \in$ $\{ P P , F P , P F , F F \} )$ . This signal has an $\alpha _ { k }$ probability of being uninformative and following a uniform distribution on [0,1], and a $( 1 - \alpha _ { k } )$ probability of being equal to the consumer’s actual degree of misfit. That is, $P r ( s _ { k } \neq x _ { k } | y = x _ { k } ) = \alpha _ { k }$ and $P r ( s _ { k } =$ $x _ { k } | y = x _ { k } ) = 1 - \alpha _ { k }$ , where $x _ { k } \in [ 0 , 1 ]$ is the degree of misfit toward App 1, indicated by the misfit signal, and $\alpha _ { k }$ indicates product fit uncertainty. Therefore, based on Bayesian updating, a consumer’s expected perceived degree of misfit toward App 1 is

$$
\hat {x} _ {k} = \operatorname{E} (y | s _ {k} = x _ {k}) = (1 - \alpha_ {k}) x _ {k} + \frac {\alpha_ {k}}{2},\tag{2}
$$

and that toward App 2 is $1 - \widehat { x } _ { k }$ . Since trying a free basic app reduces consumers’ uncertainty about the degree of misfit, we assume $1 > \alpha _ { P P } \geq$ ?????? $( \alpha _ { F P } , \alpha _ { P F } ) \geq$ ?????? $( \alpha _ { F P } , \alpha _ { P F } ) \geq \alpha _ { F F } > 0$ As product fit uncertainty $\alpha _ { k }$ decreases, the mean of the consumer’s expected perceived degree of misfit $\begin{array} { r } { \mathrm { E } ( \hat { x } _ { k } ) = \frac { 1 } { 2 } } \end{array}$ remains the same, and its variance $\mathrm { D } ( \widehat { x } _ { k } ) =$ $\frac { ( 1 - \alpha _ { k } ) ^ { 2 } } { 1 2 }$ becomes larger. <sup>10</sup> Considering product fit uncertainty, the expected perceived net utilities of the consumer with $x _ { k }$ are as follows:

$$
\begin{array}{r l} & {\operatorname{E} \big (U _ {1} ^ {k} | x _ {k} \big) = v _ {1} - t \hat {x} _ {k} - p _ {1},} \\ & {\operatorname{E} \big (U _ {2} ^ {k} | x _ {k} \big) = v _ {2} - t (1 - \hat {x} _ {k}) - p _ {2},} \\ & {\operatorname{E} \big (U _ {1 b} ^ {k} | x _ {k} \big) = v _ {1 b} - t _ {b} \hat {x} _ {k},} \\ & {\operatorname{E} \big (U _ {2 b} ^ {k} | x _ {k} \big) = v _ {2 b} - t _ {b} (1 - \hat {x} _ {k}),} \end{array}\tag{3}
$$

where $p _ { i }$ denotes the price of paid app ?? or in-app purchases ?? , and $\mathbb { E } ( U _ { 1 } ^ { k } | x _ { k } )$ and $\operatorname { E } ( U _ { 2 } ^ { k } | x _ { k } )$ are the consumer’s expected perceived net utilities of Whole App 1 and Whole App 2, respectively, and $\operatorname { E } \left( U _ { 1 b } ^ { k } | x _ { k } \right)$ and $\operatorname { E } \left( U _ { 2 b } ^ { k } | x _ { k } \right)$ are the consumer’s expected perceived net utilities of Basic App 1 and Basic App 2, respectively.

The total number of consumers is scaled to 1. Each consumer uses at most one unit of the app, which brings the consumer the maximal expected perceived net utility. Specifically, a consumer may use a paid whole app, a free basic app, or a free basic app with inapp purchases, depending on the developers’ strategies and the consumer’s expected perceived net utilities. Following Etzion and Pang (2014) and Zhang et al. (2016), we only consider parameter values for which in equilibrium:<sup>11</sup> (1) each consumer is willing to try the basic app when it is offered for free, <sup>12</sup> (2) each developer enjoys a positive market demand for any product (whole app, basic app, or in-app purchases) that is offered, and (3) the total market is covered.<sup>13</sup> Such cases can represent the real competition between two developers. Figure 1 presents the expected perceived net utility functions and market segmentations for the four market configurations. In each one, the developers do not know an individual consumer’s perceived signal about the misfit but know their distributions.

In Case PP (Figure 1a), consumers either buy Whole App 1 or Whole App 2. Each consumer chooses the option that maximizes their expected perceived net utility. Therefore, the market is divided into two segments: the consumers located between 0 and $x _ { 1 2 } ^ { P P }$ buy Whole App 1, and those between $x _ { 1 2 } ^ { P P }$ and 1 buy Whole App 2. <sup>14</sup> By setting $\mathbb { E } ( U _ { 1 } ^ { P P } ) = \overline { { \mathbb { E } } } ( U _ { 2 } ^ { P P } )$ , the market division point is:

$$
x _ {1 2} ^ {P P} = \frac {t (1 - \alpha_ {P P}) + v _ {1} - v _ {2} - p _ {1} + p _ {2}}{2 t (1 - \alpha_ {P P})}\tag{4}
$$

The profit functions of Developer 1 and Developer 2 in Case PP are:

$$
\begin{array}{r l} & {\pi_ {1} = p _ {1} x _ {1 2} ^ {P P}, \pi_ {2} = p _ {2} (1 - x _ {1 2} ^ {P P}),} \\ & {\mathrm{s.t.} 0 <   x _ {1 2} ^ {P P} <   1, \operatorname{E} (U _ {1} ^ {P P} | x _ {1 2} ^ {P P}) \geq 0} \end{array}\tag{5}
$$

In Case PF (Figure 1c), the market is divided into three segments: Consumers located between 0 and $x _ { 1 2 b } ^ { P F }$ buy Whole App 1, those between $x _ { 1 2 b } ^ { P F }$ and $x _ { 2 b 2 } ^ { P F }$ use Basic App 2 for free, and those between $x _ { 2 b 2 } ^ { P F }$ and 1 buy the in-app purchases and use Whole $\mathrm { A p p } \ 2 . ^ { 1 6 } \mathrm { B y }$ setting $\mathsf E ( U _ { 1 } ^ { P F } ) = \mathsf E ( U _ { 2 b } ^ { P F } )$ and $\mathsf E ( U _ { 2 b } ^ { P F } ) = \bar { \mathsf E } ( \bar { U } _ { 2 } ^ { P F } )$ , we have the following market division points:

$$
\begin{array}{r} x _ {1 2 b} ^ {P F} = \frac {(t + t _ {b}) (1 - \alpha_ {P F}) - t + t _ {b} + 2 v _ {1} - 2 v _ {2 b} - 2 p _ {1}}{2 (t + t _ {b}) (1 - \alpha_ {P F})}, \\ x _ {2 b 2} ^ {P F} = \frac {(t - t _ {b}) (1 - \alpha_ {P F}) + t - t _ {b} - 2 v _ {2} + 2 v _ {2 b} + 2 p _ {2}}{2 (t - t _ {b}) (1 - \alpha_ {P F})}. \end{array}\tag{8}
$$

The profit functions of Developer 1 and Developer 2 in Case PF are:

$$
\begin{array}{r l} & {\pi_ {1} = p _ {1} x _ {1 2 b} ^ {P F}, \pi_ {2} = p _ {2} \big (1 - x _ {2 b 2} ^ {P F} \big),} \\ & {\mathrm{s.t.} 0 <   x _ {1 2 b} ^ {P F} <   x _ {2 b 2} ^ {P F} <   1, \operatorname{E} \big (U _ {2 b} ^ {P F} | x _ {1 2 b} ^ {P F} \big) \geq 0.} \end{array}\tag{9}
$$

In Case FF (Figure 1d), the market is divided into four segments: Consumers located between 0 and $x _ { 1 1 b } ^ { F F }$ buy the in-app purchases and use Whole App 1, those between $\bar { x } _ { 1 1 b } ^ { F \bar { F } }$ and $x _ { 1 b 2 b } ^ { F F }$ use Basic $\mathrm { A p p }$ 1 for free, and those between $x _ { 1 b 2 b } ^ { F F }$ and $x _ { 2 b 2 } ^ { F F }$ use Basic $\mathrm { A p p } \ 2$ for free, and those between $x _ { 2 b 2 } ^ { F F }$ and 1 buy the in-app purchases and use Whole App 2. <sup>17</sup> By setting $\begin{array} { r } { \dot { \mathrm { E } } ( U _ { 1 } ^ { F F } ) = \mathrm { E } ( U _ { 1 b } ^ { F F } ) , \mathrm { E } ( U _ { 1 b } ^ { F F } ) = \dot { \mathrm { E } ( U _ { 2 b } ^ { F F } ) } } \end{array}$ , and $\begin{array} { r } { \dot { \mathrm { E } } ( U _ { 2 b } ^ { F F } ) = } \end{array}$ $\operatorname { E } ( U _ { 2 } ^ { F F } )$ , we have the following market division points:

$$
\begin{array}{r l} & x _ {1 1 b} ^ {F F} = \frac {(t - t _ {b}) (1 - \alpha_ {F F}) - t + t _ {b} + 2 v _ {1} - 2 v _ {1 b} - 2 p _ {1}}{2 (t - t _ {b}) (1 - \alpha_ {F F})}, \\ & x _ {1 b 2 b} ^ {F F} = \frac {t _ {b} (1 - \alpha_ {F F}) + v _ {1 b} - v _ {2 b}}{2 t _ {b} (1 - \alpha_ {F F})}, \\ & x _ {2 b 2} ^ {F F} = \frac {(t - t _ {b}) (1 - \alpha_ {F F}) + t - t _ {b} - 2 v _ {2} + 2 v _ {2 b} + 2 p _ {2}}{2 (t - t _ {b}) (1 - \alpha_ {F F})}. \end{array}\tag{10}
$$

The profit functions of Developer 1 and Developer 2 in Case FF are:

$$
\begin{array}{r l} & {\pi_ {1} = p _ {1} x _ {1 1 b} ^ {F F}, \pi_ {2} = p _ {2} \big (1 - x _ {2 b 2} ^ {F F} \big),} \\ & {\mathrm{s.t.} \quad 0 <   x _ {1 1 b} ^ {F F} <   x _ {1 b 2 b} ^ {F F} <   x _ {2 b 2} ^ {F F} <  } \\ & {1, \operatorname{E} \big (U _ {1 b} ^ {F F} | x _ {1 b 2 b} ^ {F F} \big) \geq 0.} \end{array}\tag{11}
$$

The timeline of the game is as follows. In Stage 1, the developers simultaneously decide their strategies to adopt.<sup>18</sup> In Stage 2, after observing their opponent’s choice made in stage 1, the developers announce the prices of their apps or in-app purchases. In Stage 3, each consumer tries the basic apps when they are offered for free, and then makes a purchase decision based on the consumer’s updated expected perceived net utilities. Table 2 summarizes the notations used in this paper.

## 4 Equilibrium Analyses

Solving the first order conditions of the profit functions, we obtain the equilibrium results for the four possible market configurations, as shown in Tables 3-5. In this section, we first examine the impacts of product fit uncertainty on developers’ profits in Subsection 4.1, and then analyze their equilibrium strategies in Subsection 4.2.

## 4.1 Impact of Product Fit Uncertainty on Developers’ Profits

Intuitively, since lower product fit uncertainty results in larger heterogeneity among consumers, it would be more difficult for developers to capture the consumer surplus and earn profits. However, Proposition 1, which summarizes how product fit uncertainty $\alpha _ { k }$ affects the developers’ profits in each case, suggests that the developer’s profit may be higher when product fit uncertainty is lower in many situations.<sup>19</sup>

Proposition 1: Product fit uncertainty and profits As product fit uncertainty $\alpha _ { k }$ decreases,

(a) in Case PP: the two developers’ profits increase.

(b) in Case PF: Developer 1’s profit increases if Whole App 1’s quality is relatively low $( v _ { 1 } <$ $\begin{array} { r } { v _ { 2 b } + t - \frac { \alpha _ { P F } ( t + t _ { b } ) } { 2 } ) } \end{array}$ and decreases otherwise; Developer 2’s profit increases if Whole App 2’s quality is relatively low $( v _ { 2 } < v _ { 2 b } + t - t _ { b } -$ $\frac { \alpha _ { P F } ( t - t _ { b } ) } { 2 } )$ and decreases otherwise.

(c) in Case FF: developer ?? ’s profit increases if whole app ?? ’s quality is relatively low $( v _ { i } <$ $\begin{array} { r l } { v _ { i b } + t - t _ { b } - \frac { \alpha _ { F F } ( t - t _ { b } ) } { 2 } } & { { } ) } \end{array}$ and decreases otherwise.

When both developers offer paid apps, they always benefit from a reduction in product fit uncertainty (Figure 2). With lower uncertainty about their preferences, consumers who receive misfit signals of less than $1 / 2$ expect higher perceived utilities from the app, and those who receive misfit signals of more than $1 / 2$ expect lower perceived utilities from the app. In other words, consumers become more heterogeneous with respect to their expected perceived misfit, which enhances the heterogeneity in consumers’ expected perceived utility differences of the two apps. This increased heterogeneity renders the two apps less substitutable and makes consumers less price sensitive to a specific app, which softens the competition between the two developers, thereby increasing their profits.

When either developer offers a free basic app with inapp purchases, the developer’s profit is not monotonic in product fit uncertainty (Figure 3). Since the impacts of product fit uncertainty on profits in Case FP and Case FF are similar to those in Case PF, we take Developer 1’s profits in Case PF as an example to explain the results.

Figure 3. Developer 1’s Profits as Product Fit Uncertainty Varies in Case PF

As product fit uncertainty decreases, Developer 1’s profit increases when Whole App 1’s quality is relatively low (Figure 3a). Disadvantaged by the lower quality, App 1 is only purchased by consumers who receive signals of low misfit $( \ x _ { P F } < 1 / 2 )$ in equilibrium. With reduced product fit uncertainty, these consumers expect higher perceived utilities from App 1 but lower perceived utilities from App 2, which increases Developer 1’s profit.

However, when Whole App 1’s quality is relatively high, Developer 1’s profit decreases with lower product fit uncertainty (Figure 3b). Given the advantage of higher quality, Whole App 1 attracts more consumers in equilibrium, even including those who receive signals of high misfit $( x _ { P F } > 1 / 2 )$ . As product fit uncertainty reduces, consumers with $x _ { P F } >$ 1⁄2 expect lower perceived utilities from App 1 but higher perceived utilities from App 2. In consequence, some consumers switch from App 1 to App 2, which decreases Developer 1’s profit.

## 4.2 Developers’ Equilibrium Strategies

Solving the payoff matrix in Table 5, we obtain the developers’ equilibrium strategies in Proposition 2.

Proposition 2: Market equilibria

(a) Case PP is an equilibrium in which both developers offer paid apps if $v _ { 1 b } > Z _ { 1 }$ and $v _ { 2 b } > Z _ { 2 }$

(b) Case PF is an equilibrium in which Developer 1 offers a paid app whereas Developer 2 offers a free basic app with in-app purchases if $v _ { 1 b } >$ $X _ { 1 } v _ { 2 b } + Y _ { 1 }$ and $v _ { 2 b } < Z _ { 2 }$

(c) Case FP is an equilibrium in which Developer 1 offers a free basic app with in-app purchases whereas Developer 2 offers a paid app if $v _ { 1 b } <$ $Z _ { 1 }$ and $v _ { 2 b } > X _ { 2 } v _ { 1 b } + Y _ { 2 }$

(d) Case FF is an equilibrium in which both developers offer free basic apps with in-app purchases if $v _ { 1 b } < X _ { 1 } v _ { 2 b } + Y _ { 1 }$ and $v _ { 2 b } <$ $X _ { 2 } v _ { 1 b } + Y _ { 2 }$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \text {Where} Z _ {1} = v _ {1} - \frac {\alpha_ {F P} (t - t _ {b})}{2} - \\ \frac {(3 t (1 - \alpha_ {P P}) + v _ {1} - v _ {2}) \sqrt {2 t (1 - \alpha_ {P P}) (1 - \alpha_ {F P}) (t - t _ {b})}}{3 t (1 - \alpha_ {P P})}, \\ Z _ {2} = v _ {2} - \frac {\alpha_ {P F} (t - t _ {b})}{2} - \\ \frac {(3 t (1 - \alpha_ {P P}) + v _ {2} - v _ {1}) \sqrt {2 t (1 - \alpha_ {P P}) (1 - \alpha_ {P F}) (t - t _ {b})}}{3 t (1 - \alpha_ {P P})}, \\ X _ {1} = \frac {\sqrt {(1 - \alpha_ {F F}) (1 - \alpha_ {P F}) (t + t _ {b}) (t - t _ {b})}}{(1 - \alpha_ {P F}) (t + t _ {b})}, \\ Y _ {1} = v _ {1} - \frac {\alpha_ {F F} (t - t _ {b})}{2} - \\ \frac {\big ((t + t _ {b}) (1 - \alpha_ {P F}) - t + t _ {b} + 2 v _ {1} \big) \sqrt {(1 - \alpha_ {F F}) (1 - \alpha_ {P F}) (t + t _ {b}) (t - t _ {b})}}{2 (1 - \alpha_ {P F}) (t + t _ {b})}, \end{array}
$$

$$
\begin{array}{r l} & X _ {2} = \frac {\sqrt {(1 - \alpha_ {F F}) (1 - \alpha_ {F P}) (t + t _ {b}) (t - t _ {b})}}{(1 - \alpha_ {F P}) (t + t _ {b})}, \mathrm{and} \\ & Y _ {2} = v _ {2} - \frac {\alpha_ {F F} (t - t _ {b})}{2} - \\ & \frac {\big ((t + t _ {b}) (1 - \alpha_ {F P}) - t + t _ {b} + 2 v _ {2} \big) \sqrt {(1 - \alpha_ {F F}) (1 - \alpha_ {F P}) (t + t _ {b}) (t - t _ {b})}}{2 (1 - \alpha_ {F P}) (t + t _ {b})}. \end{array}
$$

As shown in Figure 4, each case can be the equilibrium in different situations.<sup>20</sup> Specifically, when the quality of two basic apps are higher than certain thresholds (Proposition 2a), both developers offer paid apps in equilibrium. When the quality of basic app ?? is higher than a certain threshold but the quality of basic app ?? is lower than a certain threshold (Proposition 2b or 2c), developer ?? offers a paid app whereas developer ?? offers a free basic app with in-app purchases in equilibrium. When the quality of the two basic apps are lower than certain thresholds (Proposition 2d), both developers offer free basic apps with in-app purchases in equilibrium. Considering that a higher quality of the basic app leads to a more significant cannibalization effect, the developer may offer a free basic app to reduce product fit uncertainty when the quality of the basic app is relatively low, and the developer may offer a paid app when the quality of the basic app is relatively high.

In Corollary 1, we examine the developers’ equilibrium strategies when the free trial cannot reduce product fit uncertainty.

Corollary 1: The market equilibrium for $\alpha _ { P P } =$ $\alpha _ { P F } = \alpha _ { F P } = \alpha _ { F F }$

When the free trial cannot reduce product fit uncertainty, Case PP is the only equilibrium in which both developers offer paid apps.

Corollary 1 implies that the developer would never offer a free basic app with in-app purchases if the free trial is unable to decrease product fit uncertainty. In the absence of consumer uncertainty reduction, offering the free trial incurs no potential benefit for the app developer, but the corresponding cannibalization effect would result in a revenue loss. Therefore, Case PP is the only equilibrium in which both developers offer paid apps.

While previous studies (Chellappa & Shivendu, 2005; Cheng & Liu, 2012; Dey et al., 2013; Dey & Lahiri, 2016; Niculescu & Wu, 2014; Wei & Nault, 2013) have suggested that a firm may offer a lower-functionality version of the product to reveal its true value when it is underestimated, Lahiri and Dey (2018) provided a new perspective on the versioning strategy when the product is not underestimated. They found that the presence of an informed segment of consumers makes the versioning strategy useful, even if the average willingness to pay does not change after product exposure.

Figure 4. The Market Equilibria in the $v _ { 1 b } – v _ { 2 b }$ Space for $v _ { 1 } = v _ { 2 }$ and $\pmb { \alpha } _ { F P } = \pmb { \alpha } _ { P F }$

Our study assumes that all consumers are uninformed, and experiencing the basic apps does not change the average consumer valuations. However, Proposition 2 indicates the profitability of the freemium strategy in our settings. That is, our research extends the contexts in which the freemium strategy is employed beyond those analyzed in previous studies. Specifically, with the ability to reduce product fit uncertainty, freemium can be an equilibrium strategy in a duopoly market, even if the product is not underestimated.

Our study explains the usefulness of the freemium strategy from a new perspective. After trying the free basic apps, consumers become more certain of their preferences, leading to more heterogeneous consumers’ expected perceived utility differences of competing products. In turn, the increased heterogeneity reduces product substitutability and softens the competition effect, which increases the developers’ profits.

## 4.2.1 Impact of App Quality on Developers’ Equilibrium Strategies

The developers continually release new versions with better performance and new features (Ghose & Han, 2014), which may result in a switch of their equilibrium strategies. Thus, we analyze the impact of higher quality of Whole App 1 on the market equilibrium in this subsection.<sup>21</sup> The results are summarized in Proposition 3 and illustrated in Figure 5.

Proposition 3: App quality and market equilibria As the quality of Whole $\mathrm { A p p } \ 1 \ ( v _ { 1 } )$ increases,

(a) the equilibrium changes from Case FP to Case PP if offering the free basic app significantly reduces product fit uncertainty ( $\alpha _ { F P } <$ $\frac { 9 t \alpha _ { P P } - 7 t - \hat { 2 } t _ { b } } { 2 ( t - t _ { b } ) } )$ and C1 holds, but changes from Case PP to Case FP if offering the free basic app slightly reduces product fit uncertainty $\begin{array} { r } { ( \alpha _ { F P } > \frac { 9 t \alpha _ { P P } - 7 t - 2 t _ { b } } { 2 ( t - t _ { b } ) } ) } \end{array}$ and C2 holds.

(b) the equilibrium changes from Case FF to Case PF if offering more free basic apps significantly reduces product fit uncertainty $( \alpha _ { F F } <$ $\frac { \alpha _ { P F } ( t + t _ { b } ) - 2 t _ { b } } { t - t _ { b } } )$ and C3 holds, but changes from Case PF to Case FF if offering more free basic apps slightly reduces product fit uncertainty $\begin{array} { r } { \big ( \mathop { \alpha _ { F F } } > \frac { \overline { { \alpha _ { P F } ( t + t _ { b } ) - 2 t _ { b } } } } { t - t _ { b } } \ : \big ) } \end{array}$ and C4 holds. The expressions of C1, C2, C3, and C4 are presented in Appendix A.

Counterintuitively, Proposition 3 implies that a higher quality of the whole app may decrease the number of free basic apps in the market when the free trial significantly reduces product fit uncertainty $( \alpha _ { F P } <$ $\frac { 9 t \bar { \alpha } _ { P P } - 7 t - 2 \bar { t _ { b } } } { 2 ( t - t _ { b } ) }$ or $\alpha _ { F F } < \frac { \alpha _ { P F } ( t + t _ { b } ) - 2 t _ { b } } { t - t _ { b } } ~ )$ , but it may increase the number of free basic apps in the market when the free trial reduces product fit uncertainty only slightly $\begin{array} { r } { ( \alpha _ { F P } > \frac { 9 t \alpha _ { P P } - 7 t - 2 \bar { t } _ { b } } { 2 ( t - t _ { b } ) } \mathrm { o r } \alpha _ { F F } > \frac { \alpha _ { P F } ( t + t _ { b } ) - 2 t _ { b } } { t - t _ { b } } ) } \end{array}$

Figure 5. The Market Equilibria as the Quality of Whole App 1 Varies

Regardless of the developers’ strategies, a higher quality of Whole App 1 changes the quality difference favorably toward Whole App 1, which expands its potential market size.<sup>22</sup> However, as the increase in the potential market size varies in different cases, a higher quality of Whole App 1 may change the developers’ equilibrium strategies.

Since consumer preferences of Whole App 1 and of Basic App 1 are consistent but those of Whole App 1 and of Whole App 2 are inconsistent, a higher-quality Whole App 1 can attract users of Basic App 1 more easily than users of Whole App 2. That is, compared with Case PP, an improvement in Whole App 1’s quality in Case FP has a greater positive impact on its potential market size when the difference in product fit uncertainty is not taken into account. Similarly, the quality improvement of Whole App 1 has a greater positive impact on the potential market size in Case FF than that in Case PF when the difference in product fit uncertainty is not taken into consideration.<sup>23</sup>

However, offering more free trials reduces product fit uncertainty, which in turn weakens the positive impact of higher quality on the potential market size. With a larger reduction in product fit uncertainty, consumers become more certain of their preferences. Thus, quality attributes play a less important role in determining consumers’ expected perceived utility differences.

In consequence, if offering more free trials significantly (slightly) reduces product fit uncertainty, an improvement in Whole App 1’s quality has a minor (greater) positive impact on the potential market size when the basic app is offered for free. Thus, the higher quality of Whole App 1 may decrease (increase) the number of free basic apps in the equilibrium market.

## 4.2.2 Impact of the Strength of Consumer Preference on Developers’ Equilibrium Strategies

The product match becomes increasingly more important in the app market, which may affect the developers equilibrium strategies. Thus, we examine how the market equilibrium changes with the strength of consumer preference in this subsection. The results are presented in Proposition 4 and illustrated in Figure 6.<sup>24</sup>

## Proposition 4: The strength of consumer preference and market equilibria

As the strength of consumer preference (??) increases,

(a) when $t < t _ { 1 } ,$ the equilibrium changes from Case FF to Case PF if C6 holds; when $t > t _ { 1 }$ , the equilibrium changes from Case PF to Case FF if offering more free basic apps significantly reduces product fit uncertainty $( \alpha _ { F F } < \alpha _ { 1 } )$ and C5 holds, but changes from Case FF to Case PF if offering more free basic apps slightly reduces product fit uncertainty $( \alpha _ { F F } > \alpha _ { 1 } )$ and C6 holds.

(b) the equilibrium changes from Case FP to Case PP if C7 holds. The expressions of $t _ { 1 } , \ \alpha _ { 1 }$ , C5, C6, and C7 are presented in Appendix A.

Figure 6. The Market Equilibria as the Strength of Consumer Preference Varies

Considering the switch between Case PF and Case FF when ?? is large $( t > t _ { 1 } )$ , we find that the impact of the strength of consumer preference on developers’ equilibrium strategies depends on product fit uncertainty. Specifically, stronger consumer preferences may increase the number of free basic apps in the market when the free trial significantly reduces product fit uncertainty $( \alpha _ { F F } < \alpha _ { 1 } )$ but may decrease the number of free basic apps in the market when the free trial slightly reduces product fit uncertainty $( \alpha _ { F F } > \alpha _ { 1 } )$

Stronger consumer preferences increase the perceived misfit costs of using whole apps, thereby reducing consumers’ expected differences in perceived utility between whole apps and basic apps. Thus, some users may switch from the whole apps to free basic apps, resulting in a loss of potential paying consumers or a decrease in the potential market size of the whole apps.

Consumer preferences are consistent between Whole App 1 and Basic App 1 but are inconsistent between Whole App 1 and Basic App 2. Thus, there are more users switching from Whole App 1 to the basic apps in Case FF than those in Case PF if the difference in product fit uncertainty between the two cases is not considered.

However, offering more free trials in Case FF reduces product fit uncertainty, which mitigates the switching behavior of users. With lower product fit uncertainty, consumers become more sure of their preferences. Thus, consumers who receive signals of misfit lower than 1⁄2 for one app have lower expected perceived misfit toward that app, which mitigates the impact of stronger consumer preferences on their misfit costs and thereby moderates the shrinking size of potential paying users.

In consequence, if offering more free trials significantly (slightly) reduces product fit uncertainty, an increase in the strength of consumer preference has a minor (greater) impact on the decrease in the potential market size when the basic app is offered for free. Thus, stronger consumer preferences may increase (decrease) the number of free basic apps in the equilibrium market.

Proposition 4 also implies that, regardless of the uncertainty reduction caused by more free trials, stronger consumer preferences can only decrease the number of free basic apps on the market in the following two situations. First, the equilibrium can only change from Case FF to Case PF for $t < t _ { 1 } .$ . With a small ??, the impact of stronger consumer preferences on the perceived misfit costs of using whole apps is significant. Thus, the loss of potential paying consumers in Case FF is much larger than that in Case PF, where the difference in this negative effect could not be compensated by the positive effect caused by even a significant reduction of product fit uncertainty in Case FF. Second, the equilibrium can only change from Case FP to Case PP. Since the two developers only offer whole apps in Case PP, stronger consumer preferences have a much weaker negative impact on the potential market size of whole apps in Case PP than that in Case FP.<sup>25</sup> Thus, the difference in this negative effect could not be offset by the positive effect caused by even a significant reduction of product fit uncertainty in Case FP.

## 4.2.3 Multiple Equilibria and Prisoner’s Dilemma

Multiple equilibria can prevail in some situations (see Figure 4). When both conditions in Proposition 2a and Proposition 2d hold, two equilibria are feasible: an equilibrium in which the two developers offer paid apps and another in which they offer free basic apps with inapp purchases. When both conditions in Proposition 2b and Proposition 2c hold, each developer finds it optimal to offer a free basic app with in-app purchases when their rival offers a paid app. In Corollary 2, we analyze the multiple equilibria by examining which equilibrium can be payoff dominated.

## Corollary 2: Dominant equilibria

(a) When both Case PP and Case FF are equilibria, (i) if $v _ { 1 b } > \hat { Z } _ { 1 }$ and $v _ { 2 b } > \hat { Z } _ { 2 }$ , Case PP is the dominant equilibrium, where the two developers earn greater profits in Case PP; (ii) if $v _ { 1 b } < \hat { Z } _ { 1 }$ and $v _ { 2 b } < \hat { Z } _ { 2 }$ Case FF is the dominant equilibrium, where the two developers earn greater profits in Case FF; (iii) otherwise, there is no dominant equilibrium.

(b) When both Case PF and Case FP are equilibria, (i) if $v _ { 1 b } > \hat { X } _ { 1 } v _ { 2 b } + \hat { Y } _ { 1 }$ and $v _ { 2 b } < \hat { X } _ { 2 } v _ { 1 b } + \hat { Y } _ { 2 }$ Case PF is the dominant equilibrium, where the two developers earn greater profits in Case PF; (ii) if $v _ { 1 b } < \hat { X } _ { 1 } v _ { 2 b } + \hat { Y } _ { 1 }$ and $v _ { 2 b } > \hat { X } _ { 2 } v _ { 1 b } + \hat { Y } _ { 2 }$ Case FP is the dominant equilibrium, where the two developers earn greater profits in Case FP; (iii) otherwise, there is no dominant equilibrium.

$$
\begin{array}{r l} & {\mathrm{Where} \hat {Z} _ {1} = v _ {1} - \frac {\alpha_ {F F} (t - t _ {b})}{2} -} \\ & {\frac {(3 t (1 - \alpha_ {P P}) + v _ {1} - v _ {2}) \sqrt {2 t (1 - \alpha_ {P P}) (1 - \alpha_ {F F}) (t - t _ {b})}}{3 t (1 - \alpha_ {P P})},} \\ & {\hat {Z} _ {2} = v _ {2} - \frac {\alpha_ {F F} (t - t _ {b})}{2} -} \\ & {\frac {(3 t (1 - \alpha_ {P P}) + v _ {2} - v _ {1}) \sqrt {2 t (1 - \alpha_ {P P}) (1 - \alpha_ {F F}) (t - t _ {b})}}{3 t (1 - \alpha_ {P P})},} \\ & {\hat {X} _ {1} = \frac {\sqrt {(1 - \alpha_ {F P}) (1 - \alpha_ {P F}) (t + t _ {b}) (t - t _ {b})}}{(1 - \alpha_ {P F}) (t + t _ {b})},} \\ & {\hat {Y} _ {1} = v _ {1} - \frac {\alpha_ {F P} (t - t _ {b})}{2} -} \\ & {\frac {\big ((t + t _ {b}) (1 - \alpha_ {P F}) - t + t _ {b} + 2 v _ {1} \big) \sqrt {(1 - \alpha_ {F P}) (1 - \alpha_ {P F}) (t + t _ {b}) (t - t _ {b})}}{2 (1 - \alpha_ {P F}) (t + t _ {b})},} \\ & {\hat {X} _ {2} = \frac {\sqrt {(1 - \alpha_ {P F}) (1 - \alpha_ {F P}) (t + t _ {b}) (t - t _ {b})}}{(1 - \alpha_ {F P}) (t + t _ {b})}, \mathrm{and}} \\ & {\hat {Y} _ {2} = v _ {2} - \frac {\alpha_ {P F} (t - t _ {b})}{2} -} \\ & {\frac {\big ((t + t _ {b}) (1 - \alpha_ {F P}) - t + t _ {b} + 2 v _ {2} \big) \sqrt {(1 - \alpha_ {P F}) (1 - \alpha_ {F P}) (t + t _ {b}) (t - t _ {b})}}{2 (1 - \alpha_ {F P}) (t + t _ {b})}.} \end{array}
$$

The rationales behind Corollary 2 are as follows. When the quality of the basic app is relatively low, offering the basic app for free only incurs a weak cannibalization effect, which enables the developer to earn a higher profit by offering a free trial to reduce product fit uncertainty. When the quality of the basic app is relatively high, offering the basic app for free would incur a significant cannibalization effect, thus the developer earns a higher profit by offering a paid app.

Based on Corollary 2, we further consider multiple equilibria in symmetric situations $( v _ { 1 } = v _ { 2 } , v _ { 1 b } = v _ { 2 b }$ and $\alpha _ { P F } = \alpha _ { F P } )$ . Since the two developers earn identical profits in Case PP or Case FF $( \pi _ { 1 } ^ { P P } = \pi _ { 2 } ^ { P P }$ and $\pi _ { 1 } ^ { F F } =$ $\bar { \pi } _ { 2 } ^ { F F } )$ , the dominant equilibrium always exists in the multiple equilibria of Case PP and Case FF, where the two developers consistently earn greater profits in one equilibrium. Because the developer adopting a certain strategy earns identical profits in Case PF and Case FP $( \pi _ { 1 } ^ { P F } = \pi _ { 2 } ^ { F P }$ and $\pi _ { 1 } ^ { F P } = \pi _ { 2 } ^ { P F } )$ , the dominant equilibrium never exists in the multiple equilibria of Case PF and Case FP, where the two developers prefer different equilibrium markets. Specifically, developers prefer the equilibrium market where they offer a paid app and their rivals offer a free basic app with in-app purchases. The reason behind this is summarized in Corollary 3.

Corollary 3: Profits in Case PF / Case FP for symmetric situations

When the two developers adopt different strategies in symmetric situations $( \ v _ { 1 } = v _ { 2 } \ , \ v _ { 1 b } = v _ { 2 b }$ , and $\alpha _ { P F } = \alpha _ { F P } )$ , the developer who offers a paid app earns a greater profit than their rival who offers a free basic app with in-app purchases.

Let us assume that developer ?? offers a free basic app with in-app purchases and developer ?? offers a paid app. Due to the public-good characteristic of the reduction in product fit uncertainty, experiencing free basic app ?? enables consumers to update their valuations toward the two apps. That is, the increased product familiarity helps consumers to identify the key attributes of the product category, which affects their valuations of the two apps.

However, without considering the product fit uncertainty reduction caused by the free trial, developer ??’s loss is more severe than that of developer ??. Consumers attach positively correlated values to basic app ?? and whole app ?? , but negatively correlated values to basic app ?? and whole app ??. Therefore, offering free basic app ?? attracts more users from whole app ?? than from whole app ??. As a result, developer ??, who offers the free trial, suffers more from the cannibalization effect and earns a lower profit than that earned by developer ?? . In Corollary 4, we summarize the situations where the developers are caught in prisoner’s dilemmas.<sup>26</sup> Table 6 presents two numerical examples with prisoner’s dilemmas.

cannibalization effect, thus the developer would earn a higher profit by offering a free basic app with in-app purchases. In contrast, when the quality of the basic app is relatively high, the developer would earn a higher profit by offering a paid app.

Corollary 4: Prisoner’s dilemmas

(a) When Case PP is the only equilibrium, the two developers earn greater profits in Case FF if $v _ { 1 b } < \hat { Z } _ { 1 }$ and $v _ { 2 b } < \hat { Z } _ { 2 }$

(b) When Case PF is the only equilibrium, the two developers earn greater profits in Case FP if $v _ { 1 b } < \hat { X } _ { 1 } v _ { 2 b } + \hat { Y } _ { 1 }$ and $v _ { 2 b } > \hat { X } _ { 2 } v _ { 1 b } + \hat { Y } _ { 2 }$ . The expressions of $\hat { Z } _ { 1 } , \hat { Z } _ { 2 } , \hat { X } _ { 1 } , \hat { X } _ { 2 } , \hat { Y } _ { 1 }$ , and $\hat { Y } _ { 2 }$ are given in Corollary 2.

In the first example (Table 6a), while the two developers offer paid apps in equilibrium, their equilibrium profits are lower than the profits when they offer free basic apps. That is, the free trials are underprovided. In the second example (Table 6b), Developer 1 offers a paid app and Developer 2 offers a free basic app with in-app purchases in equilibrium. However, their equilibrium profits are lower than the profits when Developer 1 offers a free basic app with in-app purchases and Developer 2 offers a paid app. That is, the free trial is misplaced.

## 5 Extensions

In this section, we extend the main model in four directions. In Subsection 5.1, we consider a hybrid of freemium and advertising strategy, where the developer may offer an ad-supported free basic app with in-app purchases of extra features and adremoving services. In Subsection 5.2, we assume that a consumer incurs a learning cost when using one app for the first time. In Subsection 5.3, experiencing the basic apps is assumed to increase or decrease the average consumer valuations. In Subsection 5.4, the market is assumed to be composed of uninformed consumers and informed consumers.

## 5.1 A Hybrid of Freemium and Advertising Strategies

In addition to the difference in functionalities and features, the free basic app and the paid whole app may also be different in terms of the amount of advertising. For example, on the popular game app Makeup Artist, users can enjoy the basic themes for free but have to endure ads, and they can pay for the in-app purchases to unlock VIP themes and remove all ads. Such a business model is popular in the app market, which is actually a hybrid of freemium and advertising strategies.

In this subsection, we consider situations where the competing developers choose one of the two following strategies: (1) Offering an ad-free paid app, under which users pay to enjoy the basic functionalities as well as the premium features without ads; (2) offering an ad-supported free basic app with in-app purchases of extra features and ad-removing services, i.e., adopting the hybrid strategy referred to above, where the basic functionalities are offered for free with ads and the premium features and ad-removing services are sold as in-app purchases.

The former strategy is totally identical to that in the main model; thus, the consumer’s utility and the developer’s profit could be computed in the same way. However, the latter strategy is different from that in the main model, where the consumer’s utility toward the basic app would be reduced by the ad annoyance and the developer can earn from selling both ads and inapp purchases. Considering the ad nuisance cost ??, the consumer’s expected perceived utilities toward the adsupported basic apps are:

$$
\begin{array}{r l} & {\mathrm{E} \big (\widehat {U} _ {1 b} ^ {k} | x _ {k} \big) = v _ {1 b} - c - t _ {b} \widehat {x} _ {k},} \\ & {\mathrm{E} \big (\widehat {U} _ {2 b} ^ {k} | x _ {k} \big) = v _ {2 b} - c - t _ {b} (1 - \widehat {x} _ {k}),} \end{array}\tag{12}
$$

where ?? is uniformly distributed on [0, ??̅] . The consumer’s expected perceived utility toward the adfree whole apps could be computed with $\operatorname { E } ( U _ { 1 } ^ { k } | x _ { k } )$ and $\operatorname { E } ( U _ { 2 } ^ { k } | x _ { k } )$ in Equation (3). The developer’s profit is composed with two parts: (1) the profit earned from consumers, where consumers using the whole app pay for the in-app purchases; (2) the profit earned from advertisers, where advertisers pay for the attention of consumers using the basic app. Thus, the developer’s profit is:

$$
\pi_ {i} = p _ {i} d _ {i} + a n _ {i} d _ {i b},\tag{13}
$$

where ?? is the ad revenue rate, $n _ { i }$ is the number of ads embedded in app ?? and $d _ { i b }$ is the number of consumers using basic app ??.

Based on the equilibrium results of the four possible configurations, we study how product fit uncertainty affects the developer’s profit. Similar to the main model, when either developer offers a free basic app with in-app purchases, the one offering a paid whole app favors lower product fit uncertainty when the whole app’s quality is relatively low but prefers higher product fit uncertainty when the whole app’s quality is relatively high. For developers adopting the hybrid strategy, although the impact of product fit uncertainty on the developer’s profit is different from that in the main model, the rationales are the same.

We also examine how the ad revenue rate, basic app quality, whole app quality, and the strength of consumer preference affect developers’ equilibrium strategies. Consistent with our intuition, a higher ad revenue rate may increase the number of free basic apps in the market. Although the role of product fit uncertainty is not as clear as in the main model when the impact of basic app’s quality, the whole app’s quality, and the strength of consumer preference on the market equilibrium is examined, we analyze the results based on identical rationales to the main model. Detailed results and analysis of this extended model are presented in Appendix B.

## 5.2 Consumers with Learning Costs

To relax the assumption that each consumer is willing to try both basic apps when they are offered for free, we model consumers incurring the learning cost ?? when using one app for the first time. In this setup, the decision process of consumers becomes complicated. For example, the decision process of consumers in Case FF contains three steps, illustrated in Figure C1 in Appendix C. In Step 1, each consumer compares net utilities of the two basic apps

$$
\begin{array}{r l} & {\operatorname{E} \big (U _ {1 b} ^ {P P} | x _ {P P} \big) = v _ {1 b} - t _ {b} \hat {x} _ {P P} - s,} \\ & {\operatorname{E} \big (U _ {2 b} ^ {P P} | x _ {P P} \big) = v _ {2 b} - t _ {b} (1 - \hat {x} _ {P P}) - s} \end{array}\tag{14}
$$

to choose one app for a free trial. In Step 2, after trying the basic app, consumers get a new signal of misfit and update their expected perceived net utilities. For consumers trying Basic App 1 in Step 1, each consumer compares the net utilities of the basic apps and whole apps

$$
\begin{array}{r l} & {\mathrm{E} (U _ {1} ^ {F P} | x _ {F P}) = v _ {1} - t \hat {x} _ {F P} - p _ {1},} \\ & {\mathrm{E} (U _ {1 b} ^ {F P} | x _ {F P}) = v _ {1 b} - t _ {b} \hat {x} _ {F P},} \\ & {\mathrm{E} (U _ {2 b} ^ {F P} | x _ {F P}) = v _ {2 b} - t _ {b} (1 - \hat {x} _ {F P}) - s,} \\ & {\mathrm{E} (U _ {2} ^ {F P} | x _ {F P}) = v _ {2} - t (1 - \hat {x} _ {F P}) - p _ {2} - s} \end{array}\tag{15}
$$

to decide to buy In-App Purchases 1, to use Basic App 1, or to try Basic App 2. For consumers trying the Basic App 2 in Step 1, each of them compares net utilities of basic apps and whole apps

$$
\begin{array}{r l} & {\mathrm{E} (U _ {1} ^ {P F} | x _ {P F}) = v _ {1} - t \hat {x} _ {P F} - p _ {1} - s,} \\ & {\mathrm{E} (U _ {1 b} ^ {P F} | x _ {P F}) = v _ {1 b} - t _ {b} \hat {x} _ {P F} - s,} \\ & {\mathrm{E} (U _ {2 b} ^ {P F} | x _ {P F}) = v _ {2 b} - t _ {b} (1 - \hat {x} _ {P F}),} \\ & {\mathrm{E} (U _ {2} ^ {P F} | x _ {P F}) = v _ {2} - t (1 - \hat {x} _ {P F}) - p _ {2}} \end{array}\tag{16}
$$

to decide to buy In-App Purchases 2, to use Basic App 2, or to try Basic App 1. In Step 3, for consumers trying both of the two basic apps in prior two steps, each of them makes decisions regarding buying the in-app purchases or using the basic app based on updated utilities:

$$
\begin{array}{r l} & {\mathrm{E} (U _ {1} ^ {F F} | x _ {F F}) = v _ {1} - t \hat {x} _ {F F} - p _ {1},} \\ & {\mathrm{E} (U _ {1 b} ^ {F F} | x _ {F F}) = v _ {1 b} - t _ {b} \hat {x} _ {F F},} \\ & {\mathrm{E} (U _ {2 b} ^ {F F} | x _ {F F}) = v _ {2 b} - t _ {b} (1 - \hat {x} _ {F F}),} \\ & {\mathrm{E} (U _ {2} ^ {F F} | x _ {F F}) = v _ {2} - t (1 - \hat {x} _ {F F}) - p _ {2}.} \end{array}\tag{17}
$$

We present more details about the consumer decisionmaking process and equilibrium results of the four cases in Appendix C. Resorting to the numerical analysis, we find that the primary results of the main model hold qualitatively in this extended model. When both developers offer a free basic app with in-app purchases, as the product fit uncertainty decreases, the developer’s profit increases if the whole app quality is relatively low but decreases otherwise. We also illustrate the developers’ equilibrium strategies in Figure C3, which are qualitatively consistent with those in Figure 4.

## 5.3 Underestimated / Overestimated Apps

To relax the assumption that experiencing basic apps does not change the average consumer valuations, this subsection models that experiencing the basic apps may increase or decrease average consumer valuations, i.e., the apps are underestimated or overestimated.

Following Jiang and Guo (2015), the density function of consumer misfit distribution is modeled as

$$
f (x) = \theta + 2 x (1 - \theta),\tag{18}
$$

where $0 \leq x \leq 1$ and $0 \leq \theta \leq 1 . \theta = 1$ corresponds to the situations of the main model, and $0 \leq \theta < 1$ corresponds to the situations where more consumers have a higher degree of misfit to $\mathrm { A p p } 1 . ^ { 2 7 }$ We assume that consumers do not know the accurate misfit distribution but believe that the misfit is uniformly distributed on [0,1].<sup>28</sup> Thus, a consumer’s expected perceived degree of misfit toward App 1 could also be computed with Equation (2). As product fit uncertainty $\alpha _ { k }$ decreases, the mean of the consumer’s expected perceived degree of misfit

$$
\begin{array}{r} \mathrm{E} (\hat {x} _ {k}) = (1 - \alpha_ {k}) \mathrm{E} (x _ {k}) + \frac {\alpha_ {k}}{2} \\ = \frac {4 - \theta + (\theta - 1) \alpha_ {k}}{6} \end{array}\tag{19}
$$

increases. That is, experiencing the basic apps decreases the average consumer valuations of App 1 but increases the average consumer valuations of App 2. In the presence of product fit uncertainty, App 1 is overestimated and App 2 is underestimated.

Resorting to the numerical analysis, we find that the results in this extended model are qualitatively consistent with those in the main model. When either developer offers a free basic app with in-app purchases, their profits are not monotonic in product fit uncertainty. The developer with the higher-quality app prefers higher product fit uncertainty, whereas the developer with the lower-quality app favors lower product fit uncertainty. Besides, the developers’ equilibrium strategies in the $v _ { 1 b } \texttt { - } v _ { 2 b }$ space are qualitatively consistent with those in Figure 4. More details about the results are presented in Appendix D.

## 5.4 Informed Consumers

To relax the assumption that all consumers are uninformed about their own misfit, we model that a γ fraction of them are uninformed but a (1 − γ) fraction of them are informed in this subsection. For uninformed consumers, the market segmentations and the corresponding market division points are the same as those in the main model. For informed consumers, we can derive the market division points with their actual utilities (Equation 1). Based on these market division points, we have the consumer demand of each product, which is equal to the sum of the demand of uninformed consumers and that of informed consumers.

We present more details about the equilibrium results of the four cases in Appendix E. Our analytical results show that the impact of product fit uncertainty on the developer’s profit is consistent with the main model. When both developers offer paid apps, they always benefit from a reduction in product fit uncertainty. When either developer offers a free basic app with inapp purchases, the developer’s profit is not monotonic in product fit uncertainty. Further, through numerical analysis, we also find that the results of the developers equilibrium strategies in the main model hold qualitatively in this extended model.

## 6 Conclusion

Motivated by the phenomenon of the app market, we examine how product fit uncertainty affects competing developers’ profits, and analyze when the developer offers a free basic app with in-app purchases rather than a paid app in a duopoly setting. Our research extends the contexts in which the freemium strategy is employed beyond those analyzed in the previous studies and explains the usefulness of the freemium strategy from a new perspective. We obtain several novel and insightful results, which can explain the empirical observations of the app market and provide recommendations for competing developers.

Our research generates managerial implications for the information disclosure of fit attributes in a competitive market. If none of the developers offer free trials, each of them benefits from a reduction in product fit uncertainty. Thus, the developer should present as many details as possible to disclose the product information related to the fit attributes. For example, the developer can provide screenshots that visually communicate the user experience or short videos that demonstrate the user interface. However, if a basic app is offered for free in the market, the developer may suffer or benefit from a reduction in product fit uncertainty, depending on whether the product has the advantage of higher quality or the disadvantage of lower quality. Specifically, the developer with the higher-quality app favors higher product fit uncertainty, whereas the developer with the lowerquality app favors lower product fit uncertainty. Accordingly, the former should keep users in the dark, such that users with uncertain preferences would make purchase decisions mainly based on quality attributes. In this manner, the developer can maximize the profit by attracting most of the users, even those with actual high misfit. In contrast, the latter, that is, the developer with the lower-quality app should disclose information about the fit attributes to the greatest extent possible.

Due to the app’s disadvantage of lower quality, its potential consumers would only include users with low misfit. With a reduction in product fit uncertainty, these users become more certain of their preferences and increase their valuations, which enables the developer to charge a higher price and earn a larger profit.

Our research proposes a new perspective to explain the prevalence of the freemium strategy in competitive markets. Previous studies have found that offering a lower-functionality version to reduce consumer uncertainty is profitable if the product is underestimated in a monopoly market. Our study extends the contexts of using the freemium strategy for consumer learning to situations where the product is not underestimated in a competitive market. After using the free basic apps, consumers become more certain of their preferences, with some consumers revising their valuations upward and others revising them downward, leading to an identical average willingness to pay. However, a reduction in product fit uncertainty increases the heterogeneity of consumers’ expected perceived utility differences between the two apps, which benefits developers by reducing product substitutability and softening the competition effect.

Our results also have implications for the adoption of the freemium strategy when the app quality or the strength of consumer preference changes. Offering the free trial amplifies the impact of quality or the strength of consumer preference on the potential market size without considering the reduction in product fit uncertainty. However, the lower product fit uncertainty caused by more free trials mitigates the above impacts. Therefore, as the app quality or strength of consumer preference changes, the developers may switch their strategies depending on the reduction in product fit uncertainty caused by the free trial. Specifically, higher app quality or lower strength of consumer preference may decrease the number of free basic apps in the market when offering the free trial significantly reduces product fit uncertainty, but it may increase the number of free basic apps in the market when the free trial only slightly reduces product fit uncertainty.

This finding explains why SwiftKey Keyboard switched from offering a paid app to offering a free basic app with in-app purchases. The obscure app was initially offered as a paid app, with users paying \$3.99 to download it. After several updates, the app with richer features has become a global best-selling app on Google Play. In a mature market of keyboard apps, consumers are knowledgeable about this product category. Thus, offering a free trial would only slightly reduce consumer uncertainty about preferences. As a result, the developer with a higher-quality app will resort to offering a free basic app and will obtain revenue from the sale of keyboard themes.

Our finding is also consistent with another empirical observation. With the increased number of apps in recent years, consumer preferences have also become stronger. According to the Top Grossing List of Apple’s App Store on September 15, 2019, the freemium strategy is adopted by 97.6% of developers in the game category but only 43% of developers in the weather category. With multiple characters and various activities in apps in the games category, the free trial can significantly reduce consumer uncertainty about preferences. However, in apps in the weather category, the product differentiation of the fit attributes is primarily reflected by the user interface. Thus, product fit uncertainty can be effectively reduced through a detailed introduction on the product page and would only be slightly reduced further with a free trial.

These findings also provide practitioners with prescriptive insights about the adoption of the freemium strategy. First, given product fit uncertainty among consumers, developers in competitive markets should take the freemium strategy into serious consideration. Even in situations where the product is not underestimated, offering a free trial can be a good strategy to help consumers perceive product differences in the fit dimension, which may lead to higher profits for the competing developer. Second, the developer should balance the competition-softening effect and the cannibalization effect to make the decision to adopt the freemium strategy. Offering a free basic app reduces product fit uncertainty at the cost of cannibalizing the consumer demand for the whole app. Thus, in the presence of product fit uncertainty, the developer should adopt the freemium strategy when the quality of the basic app is relatively low or the cannibalization effect is weak but should not employ the freemium strategy when the quality of the basic app is relatively large or the cannibalization effect is significant. Third, with higher quality or stronger consumer preferences, developers should switch their decision based on the reduction in product fit uncertainty caused by the free trial. Specifically, with higher quality or a lower strength of consumer preference, the developer should consider abandoning the freemium strategy when offering the free trial significantly reduces product fit uncertainty, but consider adopting the freemium strategy when the free trial slightly reduces product fit uncertainty.

This study has several limitations that suggest directions for future research. We have assumed identical product fit uncertainty among consumers, but this can be heterogeneous due to different levels of prior knowledge. It would be interesting to model consumers with heterogeneous product fit uncertainty and analyze the impact of consumer heterogeneity on the equilibrium strategies. In addition, we focus on experience-based consumer learning but ignore the network effect (Cheng and Liu, 2012) and word-ofmouth effect (Liu et al., 2011). Considering all the above factors in one model can provide more useful insights for the adoption of the freemium strategy. Another interesting research direction would be to include the optimal decision regarding app quality in our model, which could offer more guidelines for the app design. Moreover, to further expand our study, future research could focus on situations where consumers have a nonidentical but relevant misfit degree of basic apps and whole apps.

## Appendix A: Main Model

## A.1 Assumption 1

We consider only parameter values for which in equilibrium: (1) each consumer is willing to try the basic app when it is offered for free, (2) each developer has a positive market demand for any product (whole app, basic app, or in-app purchases) that is offered, and (3) the total market is covered. We make the following technical assumptions in our analysis.

Assumption 1:

$$
\begin{array}{l l} \text {(i)} & | v _ {1} - v _ {2} | <   3 t (1 - \alpha_ {P P}), v _ {1} + v _ {2} \geq 3 t - 2 t \alpha_ {P P} \\ \text {(ii)} & m a x \left\{\frac {v _ {2} (t + t _ {b}) + (v _ {1} - t - (t + t _ {b}) (1 - \alpha_ {P F})) (t - t _ {b})}{2 t}, \frac {t _ {b} ((t + t _ {b}) (1 - \alpha_ {P F}) + 3 t + t _ {b} - 2 v _ {1})}{2 t _ {b} + 4 t} \right\} <   v _ {2 b} <   m i n \left\{v _ {2} - \frac {\alpha_ {P F} (t - t _ {b})}{2}, v _ {1} + \right. \\ & \left. \frac {(1 - \alpha_ {P F}) (t + t _ {b}) - (t - t _ {b})}{2} \right\} \\ \text {(iii)} & m a x \left\{\frac {v _ {1} (t + t _ {b}) + (v _ {2} - t - (t + t _ {b}) (1 - \alpha_ {F P})) (t - t _ {b})}{2 t}, \frac {t _ {b} ((t + t _ {b}) (1 - \alpha_ {F P}) + 3 t + t _ {b} - 2 v _ {2})}{2 t _ {b} + 4 t} \right\} <   v _ {1 b} \\ & <   m i n \left\{v _ {1} - \frac {\alpha_ {F P} (t - t _ {b})}{2}, v _ {2} + \frac {(1 - \alpha_ {F P}) (t + t _ {b}) - (t - t _ {b})}{2} \right\} \\ \text {(iv)} & \frac {2 v _ {1} t _ {b} + (2 v _ {2 b} + t _ {b} \alpha_ {F F} - 2 t _ {b}) (t - t _ {b})}{2 t} <   v _ {1 b} <   v _ {1} - \frac {(t - t _ {b}) \alpha_ {F F}}{2}, \frac {2 v _ {2} t _ {b} + (2 v _ {1 b} + t _ {b} \alpha_ {F F} - 2 t _ {b}) (t - t _ {b})}{2 t} <   v _ {2 b} <   v _ {2} - \frac {(t - t _ {b}) \alpha_ {F F}}{2}, v _ {1 b} + v _ {2 b} \geq \\ & t _ {b} \\ \text {(v)} & v _ {1 b} \geq t _ {b} \left(1 - \frac {\alpha_ {P P}}{2}\right), v _ {2 b} \geq t _ {b} \left(1 - \frac {\alpha_ {P P}}{2}\right) \end{array}
$$

Assumption 1(i) ensures that each developer has a positive demand for the whole app and the total market is covered in Case PP. Assumption 1(ii) ensures that Developer 1 has a positive demand for the whole app, Developer 2 has positive demands for the whole app and the basic app, and the total market is covered in Case PF. Assumption 1(iii) ensures that Developer 1 has positive demands for the whole app and the basic app, Developer 2 has a positive demand for the whole app, and the total market is covered in Case FP. Assumption 1(iv) ensures that each developer has positive demands for the whole app and the basic app, and the total market is covered in Case FF. Assumption 1(v) ensures that each consumer is willing to try the basic app when it is offered for free.

## Appendix B: A Hybrid of Freemium and Advertising Strategies

## B.1 Equilibrium Results for the Four Possible Configurations

Given the two optional strategies for each developer, there are four possible market configurations: (1) Case PP, where both developers offer ad-free paid apps; (2) Case HP, where Developer 1 adopts the hybrid strategy and Developer 2 offers an ad-free paid app; (3) Case PH, where Developer 1 offers an ad-free paid app and Developer 2 adopts the hybrid strategy; and (4) Case HH, where both developers adopt the hybrid strategy.

The equilibrium results of Case PP are the same as those in the main model. And the equilibrium results of the other three possible market configurations are summarized in Proposition B1.

## Detailed proofs of Proposition B1 are given below:

As shown in Figure B1, when 2(??+??<sub>??</sub>)(1−??<sub>????</sub>) 2(??−??<sub>??</sub>)(1−??<sub>????</sub>) $0 < x _ { 1 2 b } ^ { P H } < x _ { 2 b 2 } ^ { P H } < 1$ and $\mathrm { E } \left( \widehat { U } _ { 2 b } ^ { P H } | x _ { 1 2 b } ^ { P H } \right) \geq 0 \mathrm { f o r } c \in [ 0 , \bar { c } ]$ , the market is divided into three segments: consumers located between 0 and $x _ { 1 2 b } ^ { P H }$ buy Whole App 1, those between $x _ { 1 2 b } ^ { P H }$ and $x _ { 2 b 2 } ^ { P H }$ use Basic $\boldsymbol { \mathrm { A p p } } \boldsymbol { 2 }$ for free, and those between $x _ { 2 b 2 } ^ { P H }$ and 1 buy the in-app purchases and use Whole App 2. Thus, the number of consumers using Whole $\mathrm { A p p } 1$ , Basic $\mathrm { A p p } \ 2$ and Whole $\mathrm { A p p } 2$ are $\begin{array} { r } { d _ { 1 } = \frac { 1 } { \bar { c } } \int _ { 0 } ^ { \bar { c } } x _ { 1 2 b } ^ { P H } d c , d _ { 2 b } = \frac { 1 } { \bar { c } } \int _ { 0 } ^ { \bar { c } } ( x _ { 2 b 2 } ^ { P H } - x _ { 1 2 b } ^ { P H } ) } \end{array}$ ???? and $\begin{array} { r } { d _ { 2 } = \frac { 1 } { \bar { c } } \int _ { 0 } ^ { \bar { c } } ( 1 - x _ { 2 b 2 } ^ { P H } ) d c } \end{array}$ , respectively. Solving the first order conditions of the profit functions, we obtain the equilibrium results.
